(16)

\displaystyle\langle\forall s,w

\displaystyle\colon sB^{\prime}w\colon\langle\forall\rho\colon\textit{fp-a}.\rho.s\colon\langle\exists\tau\colon\textit{fp-a}.\tau.w\colon\langle\exists\pi,\xi\in\textit{INC}\colon\colon

\displaystyle\langle\forall i\in\omega\colon

\displaystyle\textbf{\hskip 11.49994pt\hskip 11.49994pt(a)}\langle\forall s\in\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\sigma^{i}_{S}}\colon\colon sB^{\prime}\delta_{S}(\xi.i)\rangle\wedge

\displaystyle\textbf{\hskip 11.49994pt\hskip 11.49994pt(b)}\mathop{\text{\Large$\circ$}}(\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{A}})=\mathop{\text{\Large$\circ$}}(\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i}_{A}})\rangle\rangle\rangle

Pick an arbitrary
s
,
w
and
\sigma
, and then pick the
\delta
,

\pi
, and
\xi
that Equation
15
asserts
exist. Let
\rho
be an A-fullpath such that
\rho_{S}=\sigma
and

\tau
be an A-fullpath such that
\tau_{S}=\delta
. Since

B^{\prime}=B
,
\rho_{S}=\sigma
and
\tau_{S}=\delta
, condition (a)
of Equation
16
follows from
Equation
15
. For condition (b), we will
consider several cases. Let
x
be the first state in a

\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{S}}
and
y
be the first state in

\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i}_{S}}
. First, if
x
is halted, then
y

must also be halted, and no actions will be emitted in this partition
or a future partition, so (b) trivially holds. We now consider the
case where
s\in S_{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}
and
w\in S_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}
. Notice that

x
must have the same cache as

y
. All of the states in
\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{S}}
must be related
via
B^{\prime}
to
y
, so if

\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{A}}(j)\neq\emptyset
for any

j<|\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{A}}|
, that action did not
have an effect on the cache. The last action in

\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{A}}
corresponds to the transition from
the last state in
\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{S}}
to the first state
in
\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i+1}_{S}}
. The actions in

\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i}_{A}}
correspond to the transitions
comprising the A-segment starting at
y
and ending at the first state
of
\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i+1}_{S}}
. We can instantiate
Equation
14
here, which gives us that for
the transition from the last state in
\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{S}}

to the first state in
\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i+1}_{S}}
, given that

y
matches with
x
, there exists some
v\in S_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}
and some

\chi\in A^{\ast}
such that
\mathop{\text{\Large$\circ$}}(\chi)
is equal to the last
action in
\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i}_{A}}
,

y\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC-A}]{\chi}^{\ast}v
, and
v
has the same cache and
label as the first state in
\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i+1}_{S}}
. We now
argue that
v
must be the first state of

\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i+1}_{S}}
, and
\chi
must be

\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i}_{A}}
.

Note that the definitions of
L_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}
and
r-a
are
such that any
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}}
state has a unique related
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}}

state. Therefore,
v
must be the first state of

\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i+1}_{S}}
, since both must be related to

\mathbin{{}^{\pi}\hskip-1.5pt\rho^{i+1}_{S}}
via
B^{\prime}
. We can assume that

v
only appears once in the path from
y
to
v
, since
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}}

is not capable of committing an instruction twice in a single skipping
step. Therefore, the actions in
\chi
and

\mathbin{{}^{\xi}\hskip-1.5pt\tau^{i}_{A}}
start at the same cache and produce
the same cache, meaning they must be equivalent up to “noop”
actions.

Notice that the purpose of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}}
in
Equation
14
is in some sense to give us a
specification for how actions should affect
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}}
state. We can
simplify that equation by directly defining the effects that actions
should have on the cache and checking that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}}
is behaving
appropriately. That is, we would like to check that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}}
is not
modifying the cache through some unauthorized actions that it is not
emitting. This gives Equation
17
,
where
apply-action
is a function that takes in a

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}}
state
s
, a cache
c
and an action
a
, and produces
c

after applying
a
to it.

(17)

\langle\forall s,u\in S_{\texttt{MA-IC-A}},a\in A\colon s\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC-A}]{a}u\colon\textbf{cache}_{u}=\textit{apply-action}(s,a)\rangle

6.1.
Proof Obligations for Spectre

We now describe the proof obligations that arise from using our
notion of correctness for Spectre on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}}
and

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC-A}}}
. First, we will decompose

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}\lesssim_{\textit{r-a}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
using the same
approach that we used for

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}\lesssim_{\textit{r-ic}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
in
Section
4
. The only new obligation is
Equation
17
.

Several of the proof obligations for

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}\lesssim_{\textit{r-a}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
are identical to
those that are needed here. The main differences appear in the context
of witness skipping refinement, as our refinement map and

run
witness function both differ.

B_{\textit{ic-a}}\subseteq S_{\textit{IC}}\times S_{\textit{IC}}
is the witness
skipping relation over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\textit{IC}}}
that we must show exists and satisfies
the below properties,
Equations
18
-
20
.

\textit{run-ic-c}(w,s,u)
is a function that steps
w

\textit{skip-wit-ic}(s,u)
times, using
s
and
u
to resolve
nondeterminism when there are multiple successors to the
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}

state. Unlike
run-ic
, it does not update the
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
’s
cache prior to execution, only afterwards. This is because the
refinement map includes the cache, meaning that the caches of
w
and

s
are already known to be identical.

Notice that we apply
r-a
to states in
S_{\texttt{MA-G-IC}}
, despite the
fact that
r-a
is defined over members of
S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}
. This is
shorthand for applying
r-a
to the
S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}
component of the

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-G-IC}}}
state.

(18)

\displaystyle\langle\forall s\in S_{\texttt{MA-G-IC}}::sB_{\textit{ic-a}}\textit{r-a}.s\rangle

(19)

\displaystyle\langle\forall w,s,u\colon sB_{\textit{ic-a}}w\wedge s\xrightarrow[\textit{ic}]{}u\colon w\xrightarrow[\textit{ic}]{}^{\textit{skip-wit-ic}(s,u)}\textit{run-ic-c}(w,s,u)\rangle

(20)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\forall s,u,w\in S_{\textit{ic}}:sB_{\textit{ic}}w\,\wedge\,s\xrightarrow[\textit{ic}]{}u:&\\
&\text{\quad}(uB_{\textit{ic-a}}w\,\wedge\,\textit{stutter-wit-ic}(u,w)<\textit{stutter-wit-ic}(s,w))\ \vee&\\
&\text{\quad}uB_{\textit{ic-a}}(\textit{run-ic-c}(w,s,u))&\end{aligned}

7.
Evaluation and Lightweight Verification

We evaluated our notions of correctness on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
.
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
is vulnerable to both Meltdown and Spectre attacks, so
both of our notions of correctness should be falsified when applied to

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
. We also developed
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA}}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA}}}
,
versions of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
that do not have

\mathit{in\textnormal{-}cache}
. Our notion of correctness for Meltdown is not violated by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA}}}
,
since it does not allow one to query cache membership in an
architecturally-visible way.

We developed our models inside of the ACL2 Sedan
(ACL2s)
(
Dillinger et al. 2007
;
Chamarthi et al. 2011b
)
, an extension of the ACL2
theorem prover
(
Kaufmann et al. 2000a
;
Kaufmann et al. 2000b
;
Kaufmann and Moore 2025
)
. On top of the
capabilities of ACL2, ACL2s provides a powerful type system via the
defdata data definition framework
(
Chamarthi et al. 2014
)
and the

definec
and
property
forms, which support typed
definitions and properties, and counterexample generation capability
via the cgen framework, which is based on the synergistic
integration of theorem proving, type reasoning and testing
(
Chamarthi et al. 2011a
;
Chamarthi and Manolios 2011
;
Chamarthi 2016
)
.
We used cgen to perform property-based testing of refinement proof
obligations. This enabled us to find and repair several functional
correctness bugs, including:

(1)

RSes becoming deadlocked due to a race condition when an
instruction’s dependencies are forwarded from other RSes,

(2)

failures to invalidate the ROB and register status file in
certain situations where they should have been,

(3)

branch instructions using the wrong PC value to compute the
relative jump target,

(4)

differences in how
ISA-IC
and
MA-IC
handled
halt
and

jge
instructions.

Table
1
shows for each configuration the number of
functional correctness bugs and TEAs that were found. We exhibited TEA bugs in both buggy configurations.
The discovery of TEA bugs in the buggy systems but not in the safe one
suggests that our notions of correctness are useful in distinguishing
TEA-vulnerable MAs.

Table 1.
The number of discovered functional correctness bugs and TEA bugs across three configurations of machine and notion of correctness.

Config.

Func. bugs

TEA bugs

Safe (
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA}}}
&
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA}}}
) + Melt.

18

0

Buggy (
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
&
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
) + Melt.

5

1

Buggy (
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
&
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
) + Spect.

0

2

To perform property-based testing in ACL2s, one must describe what
kinds of values each free variable may take (a “type” for each free
variable). We encoded both the
ISA-IC
and
MA-IC
states as types in
ACL2s’ defdata data definition framework. Defining a type in defdata’s
DSL results in the generation of an
enumerator
—a function
that takes in a natural number and produces an element of that data
type. Enumerators are then used by cgen to generate data for
testing. Something as complicated as the set of entangled states is not
possible to encode in defdata’s DSL, so we used defdata’s custom type
facilities instead. We defined a predicate that holds only on MA
states that are entangled, as well as an enumerator that generates such
states. Generating entangled states is fairly straightforward: we
generate an arbitrary MA state, invalidate it, and then run it forward
for a randomly selected (but bounded) number of steps. We also
modified the way in which MA states are generated—instead of the
default approach that generated sparse instruction memories where
instructions were scattered throughout the address space and choosing
an arbitrary program counter, we generate contiguous sequences of
instructions and choose program counter values that are “close” to
those sequences.

We benchmarked the execution speed of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
on an
assembly program that performs naïve primality testing. On an M4
Apple Silicon processor,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
executed an average of 46,000
steps per second, whereas
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
executed an average of 2.3
million steps per second.
Running all of the proofs and tests for the three configurations shown
in Table
1
takes around 30 minutes.

8.
Related Work

A number of high-quality survey papers have been published regarding
transient execution attacks. For an overview of Meltdown and Spectre
attacks, we recommend the surveys of Canella et al.
(
Canella et al. 2019b
)

and Fiolhais and
Sousa
(
Fiolhais and Sousa 2024
)
.

Our work uses an approach based on refinement, which has a long
history of being used to specify and reason about implementations of
complex systems. See Abadi and Lamport
(
Abadi and Lamport 1991
)
for an early
and influential paper in this area. In 2000, Manolios introduced the
use of refinement for specifying the correctness of pipelined
processors in a way that implies equivalidity of both safety and
liveness properties between the ISA and the MA
(
Manolios 2000
)
. In
that work, Manolios argues that previous notions of correctness were
insufficient since they were satisfied by machines that were clearly
incorrect, like an MA implementation that never commits an
instruction. The notion of correctness that Manolios proposes, using
Well-founded Equivalence Bisimulation (WEB) and commitment refinement
maps, has many strengths: it is capable of reasoning about pipelined
in-order MAs running arbitrary programs and that feature interrupts
and exceptions is amenable to mechanical verification as it only
involves local reasoning (reasoning about states and their immediate
successors) and is compositional, enabling large and complicated
refinement proofs to be soundly decomposed into an independent
sequence of smaller and simpler ones. Further work by Jain and
Manolios extended the theory of refinement to support skipping while
retaining local and compositional reasoning
(
Jain and Manolios 2015
;
Jain and Manolios 2019
)
. Note that support for skipping is necessary to
reason about any of the commercial MA designs that were vulnerable to
the original Meltdown attack, as they are multi-issue and allow
multiple instructions to commit in a cycle. These strengths indicate
to us that a refinement-based approach has promise in enabling both
the specification of a global notion of correctness capable of
identifying TEAs and its verification.

Another approach that has been proposed for validating that hardware
is not vulnerable to TEAs is the use of confidentiality
properties. These properties state that if a program behaves
equivalently with respect to ISA semantics when run starting from two
ISA states that differ only in places that are specified to be
confidential, the program should also behave equivalently when run
using MA semantics from corresponding MA states. Notice that a
confidentiality property is not a global notion of correctness, as it
does not require that the observable behavior of an ISA and MA are
equivalent in any way, and it does not apply to all possible
programs. The framework of hardware-software
contracts
(
Guarnieri et al. 2021
)

(referred to simply as
contracts
here) provides one way to
express these confidentiality properties as conditional
non-interference properties.

We discuss three works that describe methods for verifying that MA
designs modeled at the RTL level satisfy confidentiality contracts:
Unique Program Execution Checking (UPEC)
(
Fadiheh et al. 2019
;
Fadiheh 2022
)
, LEAVE
(
Wang et al. 2023
)
and
shadow contracts
(
Tan et al. 2025
)
. These works were all evaluated
on several open-source RISC-V MA implementations, and UPEC was able to
identify novel bugs. All three ultimately formulate proof obligations
that do not refer to the ISA, using a mapping technique similar to the
commitment refinement map to calculate the ISA observation from an MA
state. This is sound if the MA is functionally correct with respect to
the ISA, which is an assumption made in each case. Nonetheless, this
highlights that the fact that compliance of hardware to a
confidentiality contract is not a global notion of correctness, as it
can be proven independently of the ISA’s semantics. LEAVE and shadow
contracts both prove properties that hold only on programs satisfying
particular conditions, unlike UPEC and our work. UPEC requires that
the user provide a substantial number of invariants to eliminate
unreachable counterexamples and while LEAVE automatically generates
certain invariants, it is necessary to provide additional invariants
to support even simple OoO designs. Generating invariants is a
challenging task on its own, and both approaches assume the
inductivity of invariants that are considered “functional” which is
critical to ensuring that no reachable states are omitted from
consideration during reasoning. This is precisely why we introduced
the notion of entangled states, which enable us to eliminate many
unreachable counterexamples without the need to develop and verify
design-specific invariants. Like the shadow logic approach, the entangled state approach requires microarchitects to implement additional
machinery (history information), but unlike the shadow logic paper
(which assumes that the shadow logic is correct) our work explains how
one can test and verify that this additional machinery is correct.
Finally, as Tan et al. showed, LEAVE would require manually provided
invariants to verify an OoO design and the shadow logic approach was
only shown to scale to small OoO designs for proofs with extremely
limited structure sizes: up to an 8-entry ROB and 16-entry register
file and data memory
(
Tan et al. 2025
)
. Compare this to our
approach, which has a 19-entry ROB, 12 registers and a memory of size
up to
2^{32}
bytes.

Mathure et al. use an approach based on stuttering refinement to state a
Spectre invulnerability property and verify that MA models with and
without certain mitigations are vulnerable or invulnerable to Spectre
attacks
(
Mathure et al. 2022
)
. The
property described in their work does not imply functional
correctness, as they use abstraction to factor out parts of the MA and
ISA model under consideration that should behave
identically. Additionally, their approach requires that the designer
provide inductive invariants to eliminate some unreachable states in a
way that is dependent on the property being proven, as opposed to our
notion of entangled states.
Other interesting works include that of Cabodi et al.
(
Cabodi et al. 2019
)
and the
Pensieve framework
(
Yang et al. 2023
)
, both of which reason about
abstract models of MAs, enabling the identification of abstract
information leakage pathways and design-phase evaluation of TEA
defenses respectively.

9.
Conclusions and Future Work

We proposed formal notions of microprocessor conformance based on
refinement that can be used to show the absence of transient execution
attacks such as Meltdown and Spectre. We described how we decomposed
each notion of correctness into properties that are more amenable to
automated verification, making use of the novel shared-resource
commitment refinement map and entangled states. We demonstrated the
effectiveness of our approach by using the ACL2s theorem prover to
define executable MA and ISA models and to construct counterexamples
to the refinement conjecture of correctness, showing how our work can
identify Meltdown and Spectre vulnerabilities in a simple pipelined,
out-of-order MA that supports speculative execution.
As far as we know, these are the first global notions of correctness
that address transient execution attacks. For future work we plan to
extend our work to handle other side
channels
(
Fiolhais and Sousa 2024
;
Randal 2023
)
and richer observer models that
prevent unwanted leakage of information, while providing a simple
hardware/software interface that gives architects the flexibility to
design performant processors and maintaining a simple programming
model for programmers.

Acknowledgements.

The authors thank John Matthews and Brian Huffman for their support
of this work. This work was partially funded by the Intel
Corporation.

Appendix A
Notation

Given a nonempty and finite set of integers
S
,
\max(S)
is the
maximum element in
S
and
\min(S)
is the minimum element in

S
. Given a set
S
,
\mathcal{P}(S)
denotes the power set of
S
(the
set of all subsets of
S
, including
\emptyset
and
S
).
\uplus

denotes disjoint union.

f:A\rightharpoonup B
indicates that
f
is a partial function
from
A
to
B
(
e.g.
,
\mathrm{dom}(f)\subseteq A
), whereas

f:A\rightarrow B
indicates that
f
is a total function from
A

to
B
. Given a partial function
f
,
f(x)\uparrow
indicates that

a\notin\mathrm{dom}(f)
(
a
is not mapped by
f
) and
f(x)\downarrow

indicates that
a\in\mathrm{dom}(f)
. Given any partial function

f:A\rightharpoonup B
, we define

\textit{get}_{f}:A\times B\rightarrow B
such that:

\textit{get}_{f}(a,b)=\begin{cases}f(a)&\text{if }f(a)\downarrow\\
b&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

A partial function
f:A\rightharpoonup B
can be treated as a
subset of
A\times B
. This set representation is defined as follows:

\langle\forall a\colon a\in A\colon f(a)\downarrow\implies(a,f(a))\in f\rangle

\langle\forall a\colon a\in A\colon f(a)\uparrow\implies a\notin f\rangle

A set
s\subseteq\mathcal{P}(A\times B)
can be treated as a partial
function
f:A\rightharpoonup B
if
s
satisfies the following
condition:

\langle\forall a\colon a\in A\colon|\{(x,y)\in s\colon x=a\}|\leq 1\rangle

If so, the semantics of
f
are defined as follows:

\langle\forall a,b\colon(a,b)\in s\colon f(a)\downarrow\wedge f(a)=b\rangle

\langle\forall a\colon a\in A\wedge\neg\langle\exists b\colon b\in B\colon(a,b)\in s\rangle\colon f(a)\uparrow\rangle

Given a partial function
f:A\rightharpoonup B
and

a\in A,b\in B
,
[a\mapsto b]f
denotes a partial function

f^{\prime}
such that

f^{\prime}(x)=\begin{cases}b&\text{if }x=a\\
f(x)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Given a partial function
f:A\rightharpoonup B
and
a\in A
,

[a\mapsto\uparrow]f
denotes a partial function
f^{\prime}
such that

f^{\prime}(x)=\begin{cases}f&\text{if }f(a)\uparrow\\
f\setminus(a,f(a))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Given a function
f:A\rightarrow B
, or a partial function

f:A\rightharpoonup B
,
\Ima f
is the set

\{y|y\in B\wedge\langle\exists x\colon x\in A\colon f(x)=y\rangle\}
.

A sequence of elements of a set
A
is a function from an interval of
the natural numbers to
A
. In this work, any finite sequence we
consider has a domain of the form

\{i\colon i\in\mathbb{N}\colon 0<i\leq j\}
for some
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Given a finite sequence
\sigma
, the length of
\sigma

(denoted by
|\sigma|
) is the cardinality of the domain of

\sigma
.

\langle x,y\rangle
denotes the finite sequence
\sigma
such that

\mathrm{dom}(\sigma)=\{1,2\}
,
\sigma(1)=x
and
\sigma(2)=y
.
Given a set
A
,
A^{\ast}
denotes the set of all finite sequences over
elements of
A
. Given a value
e
and a sequence
a
,
e\bullet a

denotes the sequence obtained by prepending
e
onto the
sequence. That is, if
s=e\bullet a
then:

s(i)=\begin{cases}e&\text{if }i=1\\
a(i-1)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Given two finite sequences
a
and
b
,
a\mathbin{+\mkern-6.0mu+}b
denotes the
sequence obtained by appending the two sequences.

Given a sequence
\sigma
,
\langle f(x)\colon x\in\sigma\colon p(x)\rangle
denotes the sequence consisting of
f
applied to the elements
of
\Ima\sigma
that satisfy
p
, in the order in which they appeared
in
\sigma
. That is, if
C=\{(i,x)\in\sigma|p(x)\}
and

\pi=\langle f(x)\colon x\in\sigma\colon p(x)\rangle
, then

\langle\forall i,x\colon(i,x)\in C\colon\pi(|\{(j,y)\in C|j\leq i\}|)=f(x)\rangle
.

\mathbb{B}=\{\texttt{true},\texttt{false}\}
is the set of Boolean
values.
\mathbb{N}_{32}=\{x\in\mathbb{N}\colon x<2^{32}\}
. That is,
\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the set of all unsigned 32-bit
integers.
\oplus
indicates unsigned 32-bit addition,
\ominus

indicates unsigned 32-bit subtraction and
\otimes
indicates unsigned
32-bit multiplication.
\mathbin{\&}
indicates the bitwise AND operator
applied to two unsigned 32-bit numbers. Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of
register specifiers. A register file is a function

\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{32}
. The initial register file
\mathcal{R}_{\emptyset}
maps
all registers to 0.

We define transition relations for TSes by providing
inference
rules
. Each inference rule consists of two parts: a set of

premises
and a
conclusion
. An inference rule indicates
that when all of its premises hold, the conclusion must also hold. An
inference rule is represented as a whitespace-separated sequence of
premises written above a horizontal line, with the conclusion written
below the horizontal line. An example is given in
Equation
21
. In that example, the
premises are
A
,
\neg B
and
C
and the conclusion is
D
.

(21)

\frac{A\quad\neg B\quad C}{D}

We refer to an inference rule that is used to define a transition
relation as a
transition rule
. The conclusion of a transition
rule will always be an application of a transition
relation. Equation
22
provides an
example of a transition rule for a transition system

\mathcal{M_{\text{{foo}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{foo}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{foo}}]{},L_{\text{{foo}}}\rangle
and a function

f:S_{\textit{foo}}\rightarrow S_{\textit{foo}}
. Equation
22
indicates
that

\langle\forall s\in S_{\textit{foo}}\colon P(s)\wedge\neg Q(s)\colon(s,f(s))\in\ \xrightarrow[\textit{foo}]{}\rangle
. Note that
Equation
22
elides an explicit
definition of the domain of the variable
s
; any variables on the
left-hand side of the transition relation in the conclusion of a
transition rule are inferred to have domains that are appropriate
given the domain of the transition relation. For example, given a
transition system
\mathcal{M_{\text{{qux}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{qux}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{qux}}]{},L_{\text{{qux}}}\rangle
where

S_{\textit{qux}}=\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{B}
and

g:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
Equation
23
indicates that

\langle\forall x\in\mathbb{N},y\in\mathbb{B}\colon U(x)\wedge\neg V(y)\colon(\langle x,y\rangle,\langle g(x),y\rangle)\in\ \xrightarrow[\textit{qux}]{}\rangle
.

(22)

\frac{P(s)\quad\neg Q(s)}{s\xrightarrow[\textit{foo}]{}f(s)}

(23)

\frac{U(x)\quad\neg V(y)}{\langle x,y\rangle\xrightarrow[\textit{qux}]{}\langle g(x),y\rangle}

In transition rules,
r
refers to a register specifier and
c
refers
to an unsigned 32-bit number. Subscript indices are used when it is
necessary to introduce multiple register specifiers or constants. We
will define the state space of a transition system as consisting of
tuples, each element of which will have a name associated with it. We
will freely use the names of the tuple elements in transition rules to
refer to the value of that tuple element in the starting state of the
rule. For example, say we have a transition system
\mathcal{M_{\text{{foo}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{foo}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{foo}}]{},L_{\text{{foo}}}\rangle

where
S_{\textit{foo}}:\langle\textbf{bar},\textbf{baz}\rangle
where

\textbf{bar}:\mathbb{B}
and
\textbf{baz}:\mathbb{N}
. Say we have
the following transition rule for
\mathcal{M}_{\textit{foo}}
:

\frac{\neg\textbf{bar}\quad\textbf{baz}>10}{S\xrightarrow[\textit{foo}]{}[\textbf{bar}\mapsto\texttt{false},\textbf{baz}\mapsto\textbf{baz}+1]S}

This rule should be read the same as:

\frac{\neg x\quad y>10}{\langle x,y\rangle\xrightarrow[\textit{foo}]{}\langle\texttt{false},\textit{y}+1\rangle}

Given a variable
x
over a named tuple, if
bar
is the name
of a field in that tuple then
\textbf{bar}_{x}
refers to the value of
field
bar
in
x
.

Given a transition system
\mathcal{M_{\text{{foo}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{foo}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{foo}}]{},L_{\text{{foo}}}\rangle
and two states

s,u\in S_{\textit{foo}}
,
s\xrightarrow[\textit{foo}^{\ast}]{}u
indicates
that there exists a path from
s
to
u
. That is:

\displaystyle s\xrightarrow[\textit{foo}]{}^{\ast}u\iff\langle

\displaystyle\exists s_{1},...,s_{n}\colon s_{1},...,s_{n}\in S_{\textit{foo}}\colon s_{1}=s\wedge s_{n}=u\wedge

\displaystyle\langle\forall i\colon i\in[1,...,n-1]\colon s_{i}\xrightarrow[\textit{foo}]{}s_{i+1}\rangle\rangle

Appendix B
Formal Semantics

This section contains the formal definitions of several ISA and MA
machine variants.

B.1.
Formal Semantics of
ISA-IC

\mathcal{M_{\text{{ISA-IC}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{ISA-IC}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{ISA-IC}}]{},L_{\text{{ISA-IC}}}\rangle
is a transition system. Let
\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
be the set of
instructions that the ISA is defined over.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}::=\

\displaystyle\texttt{halt}\ |\ \texttt{noop}\ |\ \texttt{loadi}\ r_{d}\ c\ |\ \texttt{addi}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c\ |\ \texttt{add}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}\ |

\displaystyle\texttt{mul}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}\ |\ \texttt{and}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}\ |\ \texttt{cmp}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}\ |\ \texttt{jg}\ r_{1}\ c\ |\ \texttt{jge}\ r_{1}\ c\ |

\displaystyle\texttt{ldri}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c\ |\ \texttt{ldr}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}\ |\ \texttt{tsx-start}\ c\ |\ \texttt{tsx-end}\ |

\displaystyle\texttt{in-cache}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c

We define the set of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
states to be a tuple:

S_{\texttt{ISA-IC}}:\langle\textbf{pc},\textbf{rf},\textbf{tsx},\textbf{halt},\textbf{imem},\textbf{dmem},\textbf{ga},\textbf{cache}\rangle

where each component is as follows:

•

\textbf{pc}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the program counter

•

\textbf{rf}:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the register file

•

\textbf{tsx}:\langle\textbf{tsx-act},\textbf{tsx-rf},\textbf{tsx-fb}\rangle
is the TSX state, described below

•

\textbf{halt}:\mathbb{B}
is true if the ISA is halted

•

\textbf{imem}:\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
is a partial map from addresses to instructions (the instruction memory)

•

\textbf{dmem}:\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32}
is a partial map from addresses to data (the data memory)

•

\textbf{ga}:\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
is a predicate that is
true on any data memory address that the running program has
permission to access.

•

\textbf{cache}:\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32}
is a partial map from addresses to data (the cache)

•

\textbf{tsx-act}:\mathbb{B}
is true if the ISA is in an active TSX region

•

\textbf{tsx-rf}:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the register file at the start of the TSX region

•

\textbf{tsx-fb}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the address to resume execution from if an error occurs in an active TSX region

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
is defined as a composition of two auxiliary transition
systems, the deterministic
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}}}
and the
nondeterministic
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-C}}}
. It has a single transition
rule.

\frac{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-C}]{}S^{\prime}\quad S^{\prime}\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}S^{\prime\prime}\quad S^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-C}]{}S^{\prime\prime\prime}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}]{}S^{\prime\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{isa-ic}

Let
\textit{fetch}:(\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}})\times\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
be a function such that:

\textit{fetch}(\textit{imem},a)=\begin{cases}\textit{imem}(a)&\text{if }\textit{imem}(a)\downarrow\\
\texttt{noop}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The deterministic behavior of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
is represented using

\mathcal{M_{\text{{ISA-IC}{-ISA}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{ISA-IC}{-ISA}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{ISA-IC}{-ISA}}]{},L_{\text{{ISA-IC}{-ISA}}}\rangle
, where

S_{\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}}=S_{\texttt{ISA-IC}}
. The behavior of this system
is straightforward, and can be summarized as follows: if not halted, it
executes the instruction at address
pc
in
imem
(treating it as a

noop
otherwise), updates the register file appropriately, and
then either increments the
pc
or sets it to a different value in a
few special cases (
jg
/
jge
when taken, a

ldr
/
ldri
that raises an exception while in a TSX
region). A selection of the transition rules are shown
below.
ldr-ok-c
describes how a memory load instruction
operates in the case where the computed address is accessible and

ic-ga-p
,
ic-ga-a
and
isa-not-ga
describe
the behavior of the
\mathit{in\textnormal{-}cache}
instruction.

\frac{\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}S}\quad\textsc{halted}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{halt}$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{halt}\mapsto\texttt{true}]S}\quad\textsc{halt}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{noop}$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1]S}\quad\textsc{noop}

ALU Operations

loadi
loads a constant value into a register. The rest of the
ALU operations are straightforward: the destination register is set to
the result of some operation performed on the two source operands, the first
of which is always a register and the second of which is either a
register or a constant.

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{loadi}\ r_{d}\ c$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto c]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{loadi}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{addi}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus c]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{addi}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{add}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2})]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{add}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{mul}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\otimes\textbf{rf}(r_{2})]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{mul}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{and}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\mathbin{\&}\textbf{rf}(r_{2})]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{and}

Comparison and Branch Instructions

The
cmp
instruction compares the values referred to by the
two source operands and sets the destination register to a value
based on the result of the comparison. This is used to support the two
conditional jump instructions,
jg
(“jump if greater than”)
and
jge
(“jump if greater than or equal to”). The
conditional jump instructions will check the given source operand to
determine if the jump condition holds, and then will jump to a
relative offset (provided as a constant operand) from the current
pc
if so, or will behave as a
noop
otherwise.

\text{Let }\textit{compare}(a,b)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }a=b\\
2&\text{if }a>b\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{cmp}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto\textit{compare}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1}),\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{cmp}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{jg}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\textbf{rf}(r_{1})=2\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus c]S}\quad\textsc{jg-taken}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{jg}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\neq 2\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1]S}\quad\textsc{jg-not-taken}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{jge}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\textbf{rf}(r_{1})=1\vee\textbf{rf}(r_{1})=2\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus c]S}\quad\textsc{jge-taken}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{jge}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\neg(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})=1\vee\textbf{rf}(r_{1})=2)\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1]S}\quad\textsc{jge-not-taken}

TSX Instructions

The TSX instructions either begin or end a TSX
region.
tsx-start
begins a TSX region, setting the TSX active
flag to
true
, setting the fallback register file to the current

rf
and setting the fallback PC to the instruction’s source
operand.
tsx-end
sets the TSX active flag to
false
and
leaves the other TSX state unchanged. The values of the fallback
register file and fallback PC do not matter when the TSX active flag is
false
.

Note that unlike the similar TSX instructions from Intel’s x86 TSX
extension (
XBEGIN
and
XEND
), our ISA does not
support nested TSX regions. If a
tsx-start
instruction
executes when a TSX region is already active, the existing TSX status
information is overwritten. Unlike
XEND
,
tsx-end

does not cause an exception if executed outside of a TSX region (
e.g.
, when
\neg\textbf{tsx-act}
). In this case,
tsx-end
behaves like

noop
.

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{tsx-start}\ c$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{tsx}\mapsto\langle\texttt{true},\textbf{rf},c\rangle]S}\quad\textsc{tsx-start}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{tsx-end}$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{tsx-act}\mapsto\texttt{false}]S}\quad\textsc{tsx-end}

Load Instructions

The memory load instructions have the most complicated semantics of
any of the instructions in
\mathcal{I}
. The

effective address
for the load is computed by adding together
the two source operands. If the effective address is valid in the
ISA’s address space (as determined by
ga
), the load proceeds
and the value stored at the effective address in
dmem
is loaded into
the destination register. If the effective address is not valid and
the ISA is inside of a TSX region,
rf
is reset to
tsx-rf
,
pc
is
set to
tsx-fb
, and the TSX region is marked as inactive. This
effectively restarts execution from the fallback PC specified in the

tsx-start
instruction associated with this region. If the
effective address is not valid and the ISA is not inside of a TSX
region, the ISA is halted. This is because our ISA does not support
exception handling (which is normally what would occur in an x86
process when accessing unmapped memory or memory that requires a
higher privilege level than the current one).

Note that the signature of
ga
and the way that it is is
manipulated by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
has implications for the kinds of abstract
behavior that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
will allow for later on. In particular,
notice that
ga
is never modified by any of the
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}

transition rules, implying that the set of addresses that the ISA may
access is known ahead-of-time and is constant across an ISA
execution. This is intentional, and reflects the goal of presenting an

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
that is as simple as possible while still having enough
complexity to highlight Meltdown and Spectre.

\frac{\textit{fetch}_{\textit{IC}}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{ldri}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\text{Let }a=\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus c\quad\textbf{ga}(a)\quad\text{Let }v=\textit{get}_{\textbf{dmem}}(a,0)\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto v]\textbf{rf},\\
\textbf{cache}\mapsto[a\mapsto v]\textbf{cache}]S}\quad\textsc{ldri-ok-c}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{ldri}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\neg\textbf{ga}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus c)\quad\textbf{tsx-act}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{tsx-fb},\textbf{rf}\mapsto\textbf{tsx-rf},\textbf{tsx-act}\mapsto\texttt{false}]S}\quad\textsc{ldri-err-tsx}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{ldri}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c$}\quad\neg\textbf{ga}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus c)\quad\neg\textbf{tsx-act}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{halt}\mapsto\texttt{true}]S}\quad\textsc{ldri-err-notsx}

\frac{\textit{fetch}_{\textit{IC}}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{ldr}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\text{Let }a=\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2})\quad\textbf{ga}(a)\quad\text{Let }v=\textit{get}_{\textbf{dmem}}(a,0)\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto v]\textbf{rf},\\
\textbf{cache}\mapsto[a\mapsto v]\textbf{cache}]S}\quad\textsc{ldr-ok-c}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{ldr}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\neg\textbf{ga}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))\quad\textbf{tsx-act}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{tsx-fb},\textbf{rf}\mapsto\textbf{tsx-rf},\textbf{tsx-act}\mapsto\texttt{false}]S}\quad\textsc{ldr-err-tsx}

\frac{\textit{fetch}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{ldr}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\neg\textbf{ga}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))\quad\neg\textbf{tsx-act}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{halt}\mapsto\texttt{true}]S}\quad\textsc{ldr-err-notsx}

\mathit{in\textnormal{-}cache}

\frac{\textit{fetch}_{\textit{IC}}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{in-cache}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\textbf{ga}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))\quad\textbf{cache}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))\downarrow\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto 1]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{ic-ga-p}

\frac{\textit{fetch}_{\textit{IC}}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{in-cache}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\textbf{ga}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))\quad\textbf{cache}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))\uparrow\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto 0]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{ic-ga-a}

\frac{\textit{fetch}_{\textit{IC}}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc})=\framebox{$\texttt{in-cache}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}$}\quad\neg\textbf{ga}(\textbf{rf}(r_{1})\oplus\textbf{rf}(r_{2}))\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-ISA}]{}[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1,\textbf{rf}\mapsto[r_{d}\mapsto 0]\textbf{rf}]S}\quad\textsc{ic-not-ga}

The nondeterministic behavior of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
is represented using

\mathcal{M_{\text{{ISA-IC}{-C}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{ISA-IC}{-C}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{ISA-IC}{-C}}]{},L_{\text{{ISA-IC}{-C}}}\rangle
, where

S_{\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-C}}=S_{\texttt{ISA-IC}}
. This transition system has a
single transition rule.

\frac{\textit{add}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}_{32}\times\mathbb{N}_{32})\quad\textit{rem}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}_{32}\times\mathbb{N}_{32})\quad\langle\forall a,d\colon(a,d)\in\textit{add}\colon\textbf{ga}(a)\wedge d=\textit{get}_{\textbf{dmem}}(a,0)\rangle\quad\langle\forall a,d\colon(a,d)\in\textit{rem}\colon\textbf{ga}(a)\wedge d=\textit{get}_{\textbf{dmem}}(a,0)\rangle}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC}\texttt{-C}]{}[\textbf{cache}\mapsto(\textbf{cache}\cup\textit{add})\setminus\textit{rem}]S^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{isa-ic-c}

B.2.
Formal Semantics of
ISA-IC-A

\mathcal{M_{\text{{ISA-IC-A}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{ISA-IC-A}}},A_{\text{{ISA-IC-A}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{ISA-IC-A}}]{},L_{\text{{ISA-IC-A}}}\rangle
is an ALT.

Let

\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}\setminus\{\texttt{in-cache}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c\}
be the set of instructions that
the ISA is defined over.

We define the set of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}}
states to be a tuple:

S_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}:\langle\textbf{pc},\textbf{rf},\textbf{tsx},\textbf{halt},\textbf{imem},\textbf{dmem},\textbf{ga},\textbf{cache}\rangle

where all of the components except for
imem
are identical to the
same components in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
.

\textbf{imem}:\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{I}
is a partial map from
addresses to instructions.

The set of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}}
actions consists of sequences of authorized
cache actions:

A_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}=(\{\texttt{prefetch}\ a\ |\ a\in\mathbb{N}_{32}\}\cup\{\texttt{cache}\ a\ |\ a\in\mathbb{N}_{32}\})^{\ast}

Like
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}}
has one transition rule that uses an
auxiliary transition system.

Let

\textit{apply-prefetches}:A_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}\times(\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32})\times(\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32})\rightarrow(\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32})
be a function that takes in
a sequence of prefetch virtual instructions, a data memory and a cache
memory and returns the cache after applying the given virtual
instructions in order to the starting cache.

\frac{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC-A}\texttt{-ISA}]{}S^{\prime}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{ISA-IC-A}]{a}[\textbf{cache}\mapsto\textit{apply-prefetches}(a,\textbf{dmem}_{S^{\prime}},\textbf{cache}_{S^{\prime}})]S^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{isa-p}

\mathcal{M_{\text{{ISA-IC-A}-{ISA}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{ISA-IC-A}-{ISA}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{ISA-IC-A}-{ISA}}]{},L_{\text{{ISA-IC-A}-{ISA}}}\rangle
is a transition system representing the
deterministic behavior of
ISA-IC-A
.

S_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}-\textit{ISA}}=S_{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}}
. The behavior of

\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}-\textit{ISA}}}
can be described using the transition rules
for
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}-\textit{ISA}}}
, except for
ic-ga-p
,

ic-ga-a
and
ic-not-ga
. Notice that none of the
transition rules for
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}-\textit{ISA}}}
modify the
imem
component of the state, so
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC-A}-\textit{ISA}}}
defined in this way
is indeed closed under its transition relation.

B.3.
Formal Semantics of
MA-IC

B.3.1.
Parameters

\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}
is the set of all reservation station identifiers.
\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}

is the set of all ROB tags (ROB line identifiers). Both
\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}
and

\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}
have finite cardinality, and both must be isomorphic to the
standard cyclic group with order equal to their cardinality. The
implication of this isomorphism that is used here is the existence of
functions
\textit{next}_{\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}\rightarrow\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}

and
\textit{next}_{\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}}:\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}\rightarrow\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}
that get
the successor for a ROB tag or reservation station identifier
respectively and functions

\textit{prev}_{\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}\rightarrow\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}
and

\textit{prev}_{\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}}:\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}\rightarrow\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}
that get the
predecessor for a ROB tag or reservation station identifier
respectively. It is the case that
\textit{next}_{\ast}
and

\textit{prev}_{\ast}
are inverses of each other.

FETCH-NUM

is the maximum number of instructions that can be fetched in a
single cycle. The value of this parameter must be a non-zero natural
number.

MAX-DECODE

is the maximum number of microinstructions that an
instruction can decode into.

MAX-ROB

is the maximum number of reorder buffer (ROB) lines supported. The
value of this parameter must be a natural number greater than or equal
to
MAX-DECODE
. Without this restriction, it is possible to generate
a non-halted machine that is unable to commit any instructions, since
it doesn’t have enough resources to issue all of the microinstructions
that the first instruction decodes to.

B.3.2.
Transition System

\mathcal{M_{\text{{MA-IC}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{MA-IC}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{MA-IC}}]{},L_{\text{{MA-IC}}}\rangle
is a deterministic transition system. We define the
set of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
states to be a tuple

\displaystyle S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}:\langle

\displaystyle\textbf{pc},\textbf{rf},\textbf{tsx},\textbf{halt},\textbf{imem},\textbf{dmem},\textbf{ga},\textbf{cache},\textbf{rob},\textbf{rs-f},\textbf{reg-st},

\displaystyle\textbf{cyc},\textbf{fetch-pc},\textbf{prefetch}\rangle

where
pc
,
rf
,
tsx
,
halt
,
imem
,
dmem
,
ga
, and
cache
are as in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{ISA-IC}}}
. The other components of
S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}
are described as follows:

\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
is the set of instructions that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
is defined
over, and is the same as in Appendix
B.1
.

\mathcal{V}_{\textit{IC}}
denotes the set of microoperations corresponding to the
instructions in
\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}
denotes the set of
microinstructions corresponding to the instructions in
\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
.

•

\textbf{rob}:\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
is a sequence of reorder buffer (ROB) lines. ROB line IDs must be unique in this sequence.

•

\textbf{rs-f}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
is a sequence of reservation stations

•

\textbf{reg-st}:\mathcal{R}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the register status file

•

\textbf{cyc}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is a counter that increments on each
\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{\texttt{MA-IC}}}
step

•

\textbf{fetch-pc}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the PC of the next instruction to fetch

•

\textbf{prefetch}:\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}_{32})
computes the
set of addresses that should be prefetched when the given address
is loaded

\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}:\langle\textbf{rob-id},\textbf{rob-mop},\textbf{rdst},\textbf{rdy},\textbf{val},\textbf{excep}\rangle

•

\textbf{rob-id}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}
is the identifier for this ROB line

•

\textbf{rob-mop}:\mathcal{V}_{\textit{IC}}
is the microoperation for this ROB line

•

\textbf{rdst}:\mathcal{R}\cup\{\texttt{nil}\}
is the register that the result of this microinstruction should be written to, or
nil
if not needed

•

\textbf{rdy}:\mathbb{B}
is
true
iff this ROB line is ready to be committed

•

\textbf{val}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the result of the microinstruction

•

\textbf{excep}:\mathbb{B}
is
true
iff executing this microinstruction resulted in an exception

The reorder buffer (ROB) behaves like a FIFO queue of ROB lines, each
of which tracks the execution of a single microinstruction. The ROB
keeps these lines in program order, and this ordering is what ensures
that microinstructions are committed in program order even if they
were executed out-of-order.

\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}:\langle\textbf{rs-id},\textbf{rs-mop},\textbf{qj},\textbf{qk},\textbf{vk},\textbf{vj},\textbf{cpc},\textbf{busy},\textbf{exec},\textbf{dst},\textbf{rb-pc}\rangle

•

\textbf{rs-id}:\mathcal{R\mkern-2.0muS\mkern-1.0muI}
is the identifier for this RS

•

\textbf{rs-mop}:\mathcal{V}_{\textit{IC}}
is the microoperation loaded into this RS

•

\textbf{qj}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}\cup\{\texttt{nil}\}
is the ID of the ROB line to wait on for the J argument, or
nil
if not needed.

•

\textbf{qk}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}\cup\{\texttt{nil}\}
is the ID of the ROB line to wait on for the K argument, or
nil
if not needed.

•

\textbf{vj}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the value of the J argument

•

\textbf{vk}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the value of the K argument

•

\textbf{cpc}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the cycle at which this RS will finish execution

•

\textbf{busy}:\mathbb{B}
is
true
iff the RS is in use

•

\textbf{exec}:\mathbb{B}
is
true
iff the RS is currently executing a microinstruction

•

\textbf{dst}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}
is the ID of the ROB line that the result of this RS should be stored in

•

\textbf{rb-pc}:\mathbb{N}_{32}
is the PC value corresponding to this RS’ loaded instruction

Reservation stations (RSes) are the part of the microarchitecture that
execute ALU operations, memory reads, and memory checks. When a
microinstruction is loaded into a RS and its operands become ready, it
begins to execute the microinstruction. After a number of cycles
(depending on the microinstruction), the RS completes execution, and
the result of the execution is written back to the appropriate ROB
line. Any subsequent microinstructions that may have been waiting on
the value of that ROB line are updated appropriately.

\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muG\mkern-1.0muS}:\langle\textbf{busy},\textbf{reorder}\rangle

•

\textbf{busy}:\mathbb{B}
is
true
iff the register mapped to this entry will be written to by an issued and uncompleted microinstruction

•

\textbf{reorder}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}
is the ID of the ROB line that contains the instruction that will write to this entry’s register

The register status file keeps track of the registers that will be
written to by any in-flight microinstructions. This information is
used to handle read-after-write (RAW) hazards: in this context,
situations where an instruction reads a register after a prior
instruction writes to it. If the register-writing instruction is not
committed by the time the register-reading instruction needs to access
the read register’s value, the MA must stall on the execution of the
register-reading instruction until the register’s value is available.

When a microinstruction is issued, it will be assigned to a ROB
line. Given the sequence of microinstructions to be issued on a
particular cycle and the ROB at the start of the cycle, it is possible
to compute the ID for the ROB line that each microinstruction will be
assigned to.

\textit{rob-ids-ic}:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}\times\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}^{\ast}
is a function that will do exactly this.

B.3.3.
Additional Definitions

\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}

is the set of instructions that the machine
supports. Each instruction consists of an operation plus zero, one, or
two source operands and zero or one destination operands. The
destination operand (if provided) is always a register specifier, and
the source operands may either be register specifiers or constant
values. The set of operations supported by the machine is
\mathcal{O}_{\textit{IC}}
, and

\textit{inst-op}:\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\textit{IC}}
is a function that gets an
instruction’s operation.

\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}

is the set of microinstructions that the machine
supports. Each instruction in
\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
decodes to a sequence of one
or two microinstructions in
\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}
. Each microinstruction consists
of a microoperation plus zero, one, or two source operands and zero or
one destination operands, just like an instruction. The set of
microoperations supported by the machine is
\mathcal{V}_{\textit{IC}}
, and

\textit{minst-op}:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\textit{IC}}
is a function that gets an
microinstruction’s microoperation.

rs-needed?

Not all microoperations require a reservation station. The predicate

\textit{rs-needed?}:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
holds only for those
microinstructions that require a reservation station.

\displaystyle\textit{rs-needed?}(\textit{op})\iff

\displaystyle\textit{op}\in\{\texttt{mnoop},\texttt{mloadi},\texttt{maddi},\texttt{madd},\texttt{mmul},\texttt{mand},\texttt{mcmp},\texttt{mjg},\texttt{mjge},

\displaystyle\texttt{mldri},\texttt{memi-check},\texttt{mldr},\texttt{mem-check},\texttt{min-cache}\}

reg-write?

Not all microoperations will write to a register. The predicate

\textit{reg-write?}:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
holds only for
those microoperations that will write to a register.

\displaystyle\textit{reg-write?}(\textit{op})\iff

\displaystyle\textit{op}\in\{\texttt{mloadi},\texttt{maddi},\texttt{madd},\texttt{mmul},\texttt{mand},\texttt{mcmp},\texttt{mldri},\texttt{mldr},\texttt{min-cache}\}

reg-dst

For microinstructions with microoperations satisfying

reg-write?
, the function

\textit{reg-dst}:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{R}

determines which register will be written to.

\textit{reg-op}_{1}

:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{R}
denotes
the register specifier for the first operand of a microinstruction (if
it has at least one operand and the first operand is a register
specifier).

\textit{reg-op}_{2}

:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{R}
denotes
the register specifier for the second operand of a microinstruction
(if it has two operands and the second operand is a register
specifier).

\textit{const-op}_{1}

:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32}

and
\textit{const-op}_{2}:\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}_{32}
are
similar functions for constant operands instead of register operands.

barrier-op?

Some microoperations should behave as though they are
memory
barriers
. These microoperations are special in that they should not
begin executing while there are uncommitted in-flight memory access
microoperations (those satisfying
memory-op?
as described
below). The predicate

\textit{barrier-op?}:\mathcal{V}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
holds only
for those microoperations that behave as memory barriers.

\textit{barrier-op?}(\textit{op})\iff\textit{op}\in\{\texttt{min-cache}\}

memory-op?

Some microoperations access memory, and should be affected by the
memory barriers described above. In particular, these microoperations
should not begin executing while there are uncommitted in-flight
memory barrier microoperations (those satisfying

barrier-op?
as described above). The predicate

\textit{memory-op?}:\mathcal{V}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
holds only
for those microoperations that access memory.

Note that we assume that
barrier-op?
and

memory-op?
are mutually exclusive,
e.g.
, that no
microoperation exists that satisfies both predicates.

\textit{memory-op?}(\textit{op})\iff\textit{op}\in\{\texttt{mldri},\texttt{mldr}\}

decode-one-ic

:\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
is a
function that decodes an instruction into the appropriate sequence of
microinstructions.

\displaystyle\textit{decode-one-ic}(\textit{inst})=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\langle\texttt{memi-check}\ r_{1}\ c,\texttt{mldri}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c\rangle&\text{if }\textit{inst}=\texttt{ldri}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ c\\
\langle\texttt{mem-check}\ r_{1}\ r_{2},\texttt{mldr}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}\rangle&\text{if }\textit{inst}=\texttt{ldr}\ r_{d}\ r_{1}\ r_{2}\\
\langle\texttt{m}\textit{op}\ \textit{operands}...\rangle&\text{if }\textit{inst}=\textit{op}\ \textit{operands}...\end{cases}

comp-val-ic

:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}\times S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{32}
is a function that
computes the result of the microoperation inside the given RS,
assuming the RS is ready.

\displaystyle\textit{comp-val-ic}(\textit{rs},s)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textbf{dmem}_{s}(\textbf{vj}\oplus\textbf{vk})&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}\in\{\texttt{mldri},\texttt{mldr}\}\\
\textbf{vj}\mathbin{\&}\textbf{vk}&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{mand}\\
\textbf{vj}\oplus\textbf{vk}&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}\in\{\texttt{maddi},\texttt{madd}\}\\
\textbf{vj}\otimes\textbf{vk}&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{mmul}\\
\textbf{vk}&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{mloadi}\\
1&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{min-cache}\wedge\textbf{cache}(\textbf{vj}\oplus\textbf{vk})\downarrow\\
0&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{min-cache}\wedge\textbf{cache}(\textbf{vj}\oplus\textbf{vk})\uparrow\\
\textit{compare}(\textbf{vk},\textbf{vj})&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{mcmp}\\
\textbf{rb-pc}\oplus\textbf{vk}&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}\in\{\texttt{mjge},\texttt{mjg}\}\wedge\textbf{vj}=2\\
\textbf{rb-pc}\oplus\textbf{vk}&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{mjge}\wedge\textbf{vj}=1\\
\textbf{rb-pc}\oplus 1&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}=\texttt{mjg}\wedge\textbf{vj}=1\\
\textbf{rb-pc}\oplus 1&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}\in\{\texttt{mjge},\texttt{mjg}\}\wedge\textbf{vj}\notin\{1,2\}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

to-fetch

:S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\times\mathbb{N}_{32}
is a
relation that pairs states with a number of instructions to fetch. The
number of instructions to fetch must always be less than or equal than

FETCH-NUM
, and it also must be the case that the sequence of
n

instructions to be fetched in the associated state is issuable
(described in Section
B.3.3
).

\textit{to-fetch}=\{(s,\textit{max-fetch-n}(s))|s\in S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\}

comp-exc

:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}\times S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
is a function that
determines whether executing the microoperation inside the given RS
resulted in an exception, assuming the RS is ready.

\displaystyle\textit{comp-exc}(\textit{rs},s)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\texttt{true}&\text{if }\textbf{rs-mop}_{\textit{rs}}\in\{\texttt{memi-check},\texttt{mem-check}\}\wedge\neg\textbf{ga}(\textbf{vj}\oplus\textbf{vk})\\
\texttt{false}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Issuable

\textit{free-rob}:\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{32}
, where

\textit{free-rob}(\sigma)=\textbf{MAX-ROB}-|\mathrm{dom}(\sigma)|
is the number of free ROB entries.

\textit{idle-rses}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
collects the reservation stations that are not
busy.

A sequence of microinstructions
\sigma
is issuable in a state
s
if

\sigma
is empty or if all of the following hold:

•

|\langle u\colon u\in\sigma\colon\textit{rs-needed?}(u)\rangle|\leq|\textit{idle-rses}(\textbf{rs-f}_{s})|

•

|\sigma|\leq\textit{free-rob}(\textbf{rob}_{s})

A sequence of instructions
\sigma
is issuable in a state
s
if the
sequence of microinstructions produced by decoding each instruction
and concatenating the resulting sequences of microinstructions
together is issuable in
s
.

fetch

:(\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}})\times\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}
is a function such that:

\textit{fetch}(\textit{imem},a)=\begin{cases}\textit{imem}(a)&\text{if }\textit{imem}(a)\downarrow\\
\texttt{noop}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

fetch-n

:(\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}})\times\mathbb{N}_{32}\times\mathbb{N}_{32}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
is a function that returns
the first
n
instructions in the given instruction memory starting
from a particular address.

\textit{fetch-n}(\textit{imem},\textit{pc},n)=\sigma
such that

\langle\forall i\colon i\in\{0,...,n-1\}\colon\sigma(i+1)=\textit{fetch}(\textit{imem},\textit{pc}\oplus n)\rangle

max-fetch-n

MA-IC
is multi-issue, but it may not be able to fetch and issue all

FETCH-NUM
instructions on a particular cycle if there are not
sufficient resources available. For example, the ROB may not have
enough capacity to store the ROB entries that issuing all of the
instructions would give rise to, or it could be that all of the RSes
are busy and one of the instructions that would be fetched requires a
RS.
\textit{max-fetch-n}:S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{32}
is a
function that returns the maximum number of instructions
n
such that

\langle\textit{fetch}(\textbf{pc},\textbf{imem}),...,\textit{fetch}(\textbf{pc}\oplus(n-1),\textbf{imem})\rangle
is issuable in the given state.

decode-ic

:\mathcal{I}_{\mathit{ic}}^{\ast}\times\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}\rightarrow(\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\times\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB})^{\ast}
is a function that applies
decode-one-ic
to all of the
given instructions and appends the resulting sequences of
microinstructions together in the same order, then runs

rob-ids-ic
on the resulting sequence of microinstructions
and pairs each microinstruction with the ROB line it will be issued
to.

rob-get

:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}\times\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}
is a function that finds the first ROB
line that has a particular ID in a sequence of ROB lines. That is,

\displaystyle\textit{rob-get}(x,\sigma)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sigma(i)&\parbox{170.71652pt}{$\text{if }\langle\exists j\colon j\in\mathbb{N}\colon\textbf{rob-id}_{\sigma(j)}=x\rangle\wedge i=\min_{j\in\mathbb{N}\wedge\textbf{rob-id}_{\sigma(j)}=x}j$}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

rob-before

:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}\times\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
is a
function that returns all of the ROB lines prior to the ROB line with
the given ID, retaining order. If no such ROB line exists, the given
ROB lines are returned, retaining order.

B.3.4.
Semantics

Most of the components of the
MA-IC
state are updated in
parallel. Where one component depends on the value of another
component, it depends on the value of that component in the
“current” state, before any updates are applied to it. The only
exception is that instruction fetching and decoding occurs before any
components are updated, so that component updates have access to the
sequence of microinstructions to issue. We describe how each component
is updated with its own auxiliary transition relation, and the

stepall
transition rule below combines all of those steps of
individual components together.

Note that none of the components of
S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}
change when

S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}
is stepped and
halt
is set. For brevity, none of the
auxiliary transition systems have rules describing their behavior when

halt
holds. The behavior of the auxiliary transition systems in
such a situation is to transition to an identical state. At the top
level, the transition rule
halted
describes the behavior of
all of the components when the system is halted.

\frac{\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}]{}S}\quad\textsc{halted}

\frac{\neg\textbf{halt}\quad\text{Let }n=\textit{max-fetch-n}(S)\quad\langle S,n\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{reg-st}]{}\langle\langle...,\textbf{reg-st}^{\prime},...\rangle,n\rangle\quad S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{pc}]{}\langle...,\textbf{pc}^{\prime},...\rangle\quad S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{tsx}]{}\langle...,\textbf{tsx}^{\prime},...\rangle\quad S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{rf}]{}\langle...,\textbf{rf}^{\prime},...\rangle\quad S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{rob}]{}\langle...,\textbf{rob}^{\prime},...\rangle\quad\langle S,n\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{rs-f}]{}\langle\langle...,\textbf{rs-f}^{\prime},...\rangle,n\rangle\quad S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{cache}]{}\langle...,\textbf{cache}^{\prime},...\rangle}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}]{}[\textbf{reg-st}\mapsto\textbf{reg-st}^{\prime},\textbf{fetch-pc}\mapsto\textbf{fetch-pc}\oplus n,\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}^{\prime},\\
\textbf{tsx}\mapsto\textbf{tsx}^{\prime},\textbf{rf}\mapsto\textbf{rf}^{\prime},\textbf{rob}\mapsto\textbf{rob}^{\prime},\textbf{rs-f}\mapsto\textbf{rs-f}^{\prime},\textbf{cache}\mapsto\textbf{cache}^{\prime}]S}\quad\textsc{stepall}

reg-st

\mathcal{M_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i$}}}=\langle S_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i$}},\xrightarrow[\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i$}]{},L_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i$}}\rangle

is a transition system, where
S_{\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i}:S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\times(\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\times\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB})^{\ast}
.

\frac{Q=\langle u,rb\rangle\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textit{reg-write?}(\textit{minst-op}(u))\quad r=\textit{reg-dst}(u)\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i]{}\langle[\textbf{reg-st}\mapsto[r\mapsto\langle\texttt{true},rb\rangle]\textbf{reg-st}]S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{regstat-issue-wr}

\frac{Q=\langle u,rb\rangle\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\neg\textit{reg-write?}(\textit{minst-op}(u))\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i]{}\langle S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{regstat-issue-nowr}

\mathcal{M_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c$}}}=\langle S_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c$}},\xrightarrow[\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c$}]{},L_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c$}}\rangle

is a transition system, where

S_{\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c}:S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\times\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{reg-st}(\textbf{rdst}_{\textit{rl}})\downarrow\quad\langle\textit{bsy},\textit{reord}\rangle=\textbf{reg-st}(\textbf{rdst}_{\textit{rl}})\quad\textbf{rob-id}_{\textit{rl}}=\textit{reord}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c]{}\\
\langle[\textbf{reg-st}\mapsto[\textbf{rdst}_{\textit{rl}}\mapsto\uparrow]\textbf{reg-st}]S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{regstat-commit-ready-rm}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{reg-st}(\textbf{rdst}_{\textit{rl}})\downarrow\quad\langle\textit{bsy},\textit{reord}\rangle=\textbf{reg-st}(\textbf{rdst}_{\textit{rl}})\quad\textbf{rob-id}_{\textit{rl}}\neq\textit{reord}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{regstat-commit-ready-in-nomatch}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{reg-st}(\textbf{rdst}_{\textit{rl}})\uparrow\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{regstat-commit-ready-notin}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\neg\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle S,\emptyset\rangle}\quad\textsc{regstat-commit-notready}

Let
\mathcal{M_{\text{{MA-IC}-{reg-st}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{MA-IC}-{reg-st}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{MA-IC}-{reg-st}}]{},L_{\text{{MA-IC}-{reg-st}}}\rangle
be a transition system, where
S_{\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{reg-st}}=S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\times\mathbb{N}_{32}
.

\frac{\langle S,\textit{decode-ic}(\textit{fetch-n}(\textbf{imem},\textbf{pc},n),\textbf{rob})\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}i]{}^{\ast}\langle S^{\prime},\emptyset\rangle\quad\langle S^{\prime},\textbf{rob}\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{rgs}\text{-}{}c]{}^{\ast}\langle S^{\prime\prime},\emptyset\rangle\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,n\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{reg-st}]{}\\
\langle[\textbf{reg-st}\mapsto\textbf{reg-st}_{S^{\prime\prime}}]S,n\rangle}\quad\textsc{regstat}

pc

Let
\mathcal{M_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c$}}}=\langle S_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c$}},\xrightarrow[\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c$}]{},L_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c$}}\rangle

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{excep}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{tsx-act}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle S,\emptyset\rangle}\quad\textsc{pc-commit-excp-notsx}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{excep}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{rob-mop}_{\textit{rl}}=\texttt{mem-check}\vee\textbf{rob-mop}_{\textit{rl}}=\texttt{memi-check}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{pc-commit-mem}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{excep}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{rob-mop}_{\textit{rl}}=\texttt{mjg}\vee\textbf{rob-mop}_{\textit{rl}}=\texttt{mjge}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{val}_{\textit{rl}}]S,\emptyset\rangle}\quad\textsc{pc-commit-jmp}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{excep}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{rob-mop}_{\textit{rl}}=\texttt{mhalt}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1]S,\emptyset\rangle}\quad\textsc{pc-commit-halt}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{excep}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{rob-mop}_{\textit{rl}}\notin\{\texttt{mem-check},\texttt{memi-check},\texttt{mjge},\texttt{mjg},\texttt{mhalt}\}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle[\textbf{pc}\mapsto\textbf{pc}\oplus 1]S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{pc-commit-other}

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\neg\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textit{pc}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle S,\emptyset\rangle}\quad\textsc{pc-commit-notrdy}

.

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\textbf{excep}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{tsx}\text{-}{}c]{}\\

\frac{Q=\textit{rl}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\neg\textbf{rdy}_{\textit{rl}}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{tsx}\text{-}{}c]{}\langle S,\emptyset\rangle}\quad\textsc{tsx-commit-notrdy}

Let
\mathcal{M_{\text{{MA-IC}-{tsx}}}}=\langle S_{\text{{MA-IC}-{tsx}}},\xrightarrow[\text{{MA-IC}-{tsx}}]{},L_{\text{{MA-IC}-{tsx}}}\rangle

.

rf

Let
\mathcal{M_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rf}\text{-}{}c$}}}=\langle S_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rf}\text{-}{}c$}},\xrightarrow[\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rf}\text{-}{}c$}]{},L_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rf}\text{-}{}c$}}\rangle

.

.

\frac{\langle S,\textbf{rob}\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rf}\text{-}{}c]{}^{\ast}\langle S^{\prime},\emptyset\rangle\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{S\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}-\textbf{rf}]{}[\textbf{rf}\mapsto\textbf{rf}_{S^{\prime}}]S}\quad\textsc{rf}

rob

Since
MA-IC
is pipelined, it needs to deal with situations where the
contents of the pipeline must be invalidated as they correspond to
microinstructions that should not be brought to retirement. An
invalidation is necessary on a cycle if there exists a
microinstruction that will be committed on that cycle that satisfies
one of the following conditions: the microinstruction resulted in an
exception, the microinstruction is a jump, or the microinstruction is
a halt. Let
\textit{invalidate?}:\mathcal{RBL}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
be a predicate
over ROBs that holds iff the ROB indicates that an invalidation will
be required.

Let
\mathcal{M_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i$}}}=\langle S_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i$}},\xrightarrow[\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i$}]{},L_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i$}}\rangle
be a transition system, where

S_{\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i}:S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\times(\mathcal{U}_{\textit{IC}}\times\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB})^{\ast}
.

\frac{Q=\langle u,\textit{rb}\rangle\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textit{minst-op}(u)\in\{\mathtt{mjg},\mathtt{mjge}\}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i]{}\\
\langle[\textbf{rob}\mapsto\textbf{rob}\mathbin{+\mkern-6.0mu+}\langle\langle\textit{rb},\textit{minst-op}(u),\texttt{nil},\texttt{false},0,\texttt{false}\rangle\rangle]S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{rob-issue-jmp}

mtsx-start
,
mtsx-end
, and
mhalt
also don’t
write to a register, but they are marked as ready immediately upon issue.

\frac{Q=\langle u,\textit{rb}\rangle\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textit{minst-op}(u)\in\{\texttt{mtsx-end},\texttt{mhalt}\}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i]{}\\
\langle[\textbf{rob}\mapsto\textbf{rob}\mathbin{+\mkern-6.0mu+}\langle\langle\textit{rb},\textit{minst-op}(u),\texttt{nil},\texttt{true},0,\texttt{false}\rangle\rangle]S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{rob-issue-halt-tsx-end}

\frac{Q=\langle u,\textit{rb}\rangle\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad u=\framebox{$\texttt{mtsx-start}\ c$}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i]{}\\
\langle[\textbf{rob}\mapsto\textbf{rob}\mathbin{+\mkern-6.0mu+}\langle\langle\textit{rb},\textit{minst-op}(u),\texttt{nil},\texttt{true},c,\texttt{false}\rangle\rangle]S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{rob-issue-tsx-start}

\frac{Q=\langle u,\textit{rb}\rangle\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textit{minst-op}(u)\notin\{\mathtt{mjg},\mathtt{mjge},\texttt{mtsx-start},\texttt{mtsx-end},\texttt{mhalt}\}\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}i]{}\\
\langle[\textbf{rob}\mapsto\textbf{rob}\mathbin{+\mkern-6.0mu+}\langle\langle\textit{rb},\textit{minst-op}(u),\textit{reg-dst}(u),\texttt{false},0,\texttt{false}\rangle\rangle]S,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{rob-issue-other}

Let
\mathcal{M_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}w$}}}=\langle S_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}w$}},\xrightarrow[\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}w$}]{},L_{\text{$\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}w$}}\rangle
be a transition system, where

S_{\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}w}:S_{\texttt{MA-IC}}\times\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}^{\ast}
.

rob-wrb-rdy
describes how the ROB is updated when a RS becomes
ready. Its definition hinges on two functions:
comp-val
,
which uses the source operand values in the RS to compute the result
of the RS’s microoperation, and
comp-exc
which
determines if the microoperation should result in an exception
instead. The appropriate ROB entry is updated with the result of these
two functions. In a MA state reachable from a clean start state, it
should always be the case that if a RS becomes ready, there exists
exactly one ROB line with that RS’s destination ID in the ROB.

\frac{Q=\textit{rs}\bullet Q^{\prime}\quad\textbf{cyc}=\textbf{cpc}_{\textit{rs}}\quad\textbf{busy}_{\textit{rs}}\quad\textbf{exec}_{\textit{rs}}\quad\langle\exists i\colon i\in\mathbb{N}\colon\textbf{rob-id}_{\textbf{rob}(i)}=\textbf{dst}_{\textit{rs}}\rangle\quad\text{Let }i=\min_{j\in\mathbb{N}\wedge\textbf{rob-id}_{\textbf{rob}(j)}=\textbf{dst}_{\textit{rs}}}j\quad\neg\textbf{halt}}{\langle S,Q\rangle\xrightarrow[\texttt{MA-IC}\text{-}\textbf{rob}\text{-}{}w]{}\langle[\textbf{rob}\mapsto[i\mapsto\\
\textbf{rob}(i)]\textbf{rob}]S\\
,Q^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{rob-wrb-rdy}

\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\textit{prop-single-ic}:\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muB}\times\mathbb{N}_{32}\times\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R\mkern-1.0muS}_{\textit{IC}}
